# Algeria

Country in North Africa

This article is about the country. For other uses, see [Algeria (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria_\(disambiguation\) "Algeria (disambiguation)").

**Algeria**,[\[e\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-17) officially the **People's Democratic Republic of Algeria**,[\[f\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) is a country in the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb "Maghreb") region of [North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Africa "North Africa"). It is bordered to [the northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Tunisia_border "Algeria–Tunisia border") by [Tunisia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunisia "Tunisia"); to [the east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Libya_border "Algeria–Libya border") by [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libya "Libya"); to [the southeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Niger_border "Algeria–Niger border") by [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger "Niger"); to [the southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Western_Sahara_border "Algeria–Western Sahara border") by [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali "Mali"), [Mauritania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauritania "Mauritania"), and [Western Sahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Sahara "Western Sahara"); to [the west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Morocco_border "Algeria–Morocco border") by [Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morocco "Morocco"); and to the north by the [Mediterranean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Sea "Mediterranean Sea"). The capital and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Algeria "List of cities in Algeria") is [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers "Algiers"), located in the far north on the Mediterranean coast.

Inhabited since [prehistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory "Prehistory"), Algeria has been at the crossroads of numerous cultures and civilisations, including the [Phoenicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicians "Phoenicians"), [Numidians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numidia "Numidia"), [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome "Ancient Rome"), [Vandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandals "Vandals"), and [Byzantine Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Greeks "Byzantine Greeks"). Its modern identity is rooted in centuries of [Arab Muslim migration waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Maghreb "Arab migrations to the Maghreb") since [the seventh century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Maghreb "Muslim conquest of the Maghreb") and the subsequent [Arabisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabization "Arabization") of the indigenous populations. Following a succession of [Islamic Arab and Berber dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Muslim_states_and_dynasties#North_Africa_\(Algeria,_Egypt,_Libya,_Morocco,_Tunisia\) "List of Muslim states and dynasties") between the eighth and 15th centuries, the [Regency of Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_of_Algiers "Regency of Algiers") was established in 1516 as a largely independent [tributary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tributary_state "Tributary state") of the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire "Ottoman Empire"). After nearly three centuries as a major power in the Mediterranean, the country was [invaded by France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Algiers_in_1830 "Invasion of Algiers in 1830") in 1830 and [formally annexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Algeria "French Algeria") in 1848, though it was not fully [conquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_conquest_of_Algeria "French conquest of Algeria") and [pacified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacification_of_Algeria "Pacification of Algeria") until 1903. French rule brought [mass European settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied-Noir "Pied-Noir") that displaced the local population, which was reduced by up to one-third due to warfare, disease, and starvation.[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19) The [Sétif and Guelma massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9tif_and_Guelma_massacre "Sétif and Guelma massacre") in 1945 catalysed local resistance that culminated in the outbreak of the [Algerian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War "Algerian War") in 1954. Algeria [gained its independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Algeria\) "Independence Day (Algeria)") in 1962. The country descended into a [bloody civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_Civil_War "Algerian Civil War") from 1992 to 2002.

Spanning 2,381,741 square kilometres (919,595 sq mi), Algeria is the world's [tenth-largest nation by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area "List of countries and dependencies by area"), and the [largest nation in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_area "List of African countries by area").[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20) It has a semi-arid climate, with the [Sahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahara "Sahara") desert dominating most of the territory except for its [fertile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_fertility "Soil fertility") and mountainous north, where most of the population is concentrated. With a population of 44 million, Algeria is the [tenth-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_population "List of African countries by population") in Africa, and the [33rd-most populous country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population "List of countries and dependencies by population") in the world. Algeria's official languages are [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Standard_Arabic "Modern Standard Arabic") and [Tamazight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Algerian_Berber "Standard Algerian Berber"); [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language_in_Algeria "French language in Algeria") is used in media, education, and certain administrative matters, but it has no official status. The vast majority of the population speak the [Algerian dialect of Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_Arabic "Algerian Arabic"). Most Algerians are [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs "Arabs"), with [Berbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berbers "Berbers") forming a sizeable minority. [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam") is the official religion and practised by 99 percent of the population.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-AlgeriaFactbook-21)

Algeria is a [semi-presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system "Semi-presidential system") composed of [58 provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Algeria "Provinces of Algeria") ([*wilayas*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilaya "Wilaya")) and [1,541 communes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communes_of_Algeria "Communes of Algeria"). It is a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power "Regional power") in North Africa and a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power "Middle power") in global affairs. The country has the second-highest [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index "Human Development Index") in continental Africa and one of the [largest economies in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\) "List of African countries by GDP (nominal)"), due mostly to its large petroleum and natural gas reserves, which are the [sixteenth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves "List of countries by proven oil reserves") and [ninth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves "List of countries by natural gas proven reserves") in the world, respectively. [Sonatrach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonatrach "Sonatrach"), the national oil company, is the largest company in Africa and a major supplier of natural gas to Europe. The [Algerian military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_People%27s_National_Armed_Forces "Algerian People's National Armed Forces") is one of the largest in Africa, with the [highest defence budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditures "List of countries with highest military expenditures") on the continent and among the highest in the world (ranks 22nd globally).[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22) Algeria is a member of the [African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Union "African Union"), the [Arab League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_League "Arab League"), the [OIC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation "Organisation of Islamic Cooperation"), [OPEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OPEC "OPEC"), the United Nations, and the [Arab Maghreb Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Maghreb_Union "Arab Maghreb Union"), of which it is a founding member.

## Name

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Name")]

[![Page of typeset book](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Civitates-Orbis-Terrarium-1575.jpg/220px-Civitates-Orbis-Terrarium-1575.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Civitates-Orbis-Terrarium-1575.jpg)

"Algeria" page in the *[Civitates Orbis Terrarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georg_Braun "Georg Braun")* of 1575

Different forms of the name Algeria include: [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language "Arabic language"): الجزائر, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic "Romanization of Arabic"): *al-Jazāʾir*, [Algerian Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_Arabic_language "Algerian Arabic language"): دزاير, romanized: *dzāyer*, [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): *l'Algérie*. The country's full name is officially the *People's Democratic Republic of Algeria*[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-23) (Arabic: الجمهورية الجزائرية الديمقراطية الشعبية, romanized: *al-Jumhūriyah al-Jazāʾiriyah ad-Dīmuqrāṭiyah ash‑Shaʿbiyah*; French: *République algérienne démocratique et populaire*, abbr. RADP; [Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_languages "Berber languages") [Tifinagh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tifinagh "Tifinagh"): ⵜⴰⴳⴷⵓⴷⴰ ⵜⴰⵣⵣⴰⵢⵔⵉⵜ ⵜⴰⵎⴰⴳⴷⴰⵢⵜ ⵜⴰⵖⴻⵔⴼⴰⵏⵜ,[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24)[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25)[\[g\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-transcription-27) [Berber Latin alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_Latin_alphabet "Berber Latin alphabet"): *Tagduda tazzayrit tamagdayt taɣerfant*[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-28)).

### Etymology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Etymology")]

Algeria's name derives from the city of [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers "Algiers"), which in turn derives from the Arabic *al-Jazāʾir* (الجزائر, "the islands"), referring to four small islands off its coast,[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29) a truncated form of the older *Jazāʾir Banī Mazghanna* (جزائر بني مزغنة, "islands of Bani Mazghanna").[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30)[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-idrisi-31)\[*[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")*][\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-khaldun-32)\[*[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")*] The name was given by [Buluggin ibn Ziri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buluggin_ibn_Ziri "Buluggin ibn Ziri") after he established the city on the ruins of the [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia "Phoenicia") city of [Icosium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icosium "Icosium") in 950.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-33) It was employed by medieval geographers such as [Muhammad al-Idrisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Idrisi "Muhammad al-Idrisi") and [Yaqut al-Hamawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaqut_al-Hamawi "Yaqut al-Hamawi").

Algeria took its name from the Regency of Algeria[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Nyrop-1972-34)[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-35)[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-36) or Regency of Algiers,[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-37) when Ottoman rule was established in the central [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb "Maghreb") in early 16th century. This period saw the installation of a political and administrative organisation which participated in the establishment of the *Watan el djazâïr* (وطن الجزائر, country of Algiers) and the definition of its borders with its neighboring entities on the east and west.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) The [Ottoman Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks "Ottoman Turks") who settled in Algeria referred both to themselves[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-39)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Naylor-2006-40)[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-41) and the peoples as "[Algerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerians "Algerians")".[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-42)[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Nyrop-1972-34) Acting as a central [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military "Military") and [political authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_authority "Political authority") in the regency, the Ottoman Turks shaped the modern [political identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_identity "Political identity") of Algeria as a state possessing all the attributes of [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign "Sovereign") independence, despite still being nominally subject to the [Ottoman sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sultans_of_the_Ottoman_Empire "List of sultans of the Ottoman Empire").[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43)[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENyrop197215-44) Algerian nationalist, historian and statesman [Ahmed Tewfik El Madani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Tewfik_El_Madani "Ahmed Tewfik El Madani") regarded the regency as the "first Algerian state" and the "Algerian Ottoman republic".[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Naylor-2006-40)[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-45)[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: History")]

Main article: [History of Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Algeria "History of Algeria")

### Prehistory and ancient history

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Prehistory and ancient history")]

Main articles: [Prehistoric North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_North_Africa "Prehistoric North Africa") and [North Africa during Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Africa_during_Antiquity "North Africa during Antiquity")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Djemila7.jpg/220px-Djemila7.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Djemila7.jpg)

Roman ruins at [Djémila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dj%C3%A9mila "Djémila")

Around \~1.8-million-year-old stone artifacts from Ain Hanech (Algeria) were considered to represent the oldest archaeological materials in North Africa.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-science.org-47) Stone artifacts and cut-marked bones that were excavated from two nearby deposits at Ain Boucherit are estimated to be \~1.9 million years old, and even older stone artifacts to be as old as \~2.4 million years.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-science.org-47) Hence, the Ain Boucherit evidence shows that ancestral hominins inhabited the Mediterranean fringe in northern Africa much earlier than previously thought. The evidence strongly argues for early dispersal of stone tool manufacture and use from East Africa, or a possible multiple-origin scenario of stone technology in both East and North Africa.

Neanderthal tool makers produced hand axes in the [Levalloisian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levallois_technique "Levallois technique") and [Mousterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mousterian "Mousterian") styles (43,000 BC) similar to those in the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant "Levant").[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-48)[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49) Algeria was the site of the highest state of development of [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic "Middle Paleolithic") [Flake tool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flake_tool "Flake tool") techniques.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-DelsonTattersall2004-50) Tools of this era, starting about 30,000 BC, are called [Aterian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aterian "Aterian") (after the archaeological site of [Bir el Ater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bir_el_Ater "Bir el Ater"), south of [Tebessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%A9bessa "Tébessa")).

The earliest blade industries in North Africa are called [Iberomaurusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberomaurusian "Iberomaurusian") (located mainly in the [Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oran "Oran") region). This industry appears to have spread throughout the coastal regions of the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb "Maghreb") between 15,000 and 10,000 BC. [Neolithic civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic "Neolithic") (animal domestication and agriculture) developed in the Saharan and Mediterranean Maghreb perhaps as early as 11,000 BC[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-51) or as late as between 6000 and 2000 BC. This life, richly depicted in the [Tassili n'Ajjer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tassili_n%27Ajjer "Tassili n'Ajjer") paintings, predominated in Algeria until the classical period. The mixture of peoples of North Africa coalesced eventually into a distinct native population that came to be called [Berbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people "Berber people"), who are the indigenous peoples of northern Africa.[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-52)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Timgad%2C_Algeria_-_panoramio_%2820%29.jpg/220px-Timgad%2C_Algeria_-_panoramio_%2820%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Timgad,_Algeria_-_panoramio_\(20\).jpg)

Ancient [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire "Roman Empire") ruins of [Timgad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timgad "Timgad") on the street leading to the local [Arch of Trajan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Trajan_\(Timgad\) "Arch of Trajan (Timgad)")

From their principal center of power at [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage "Carthage"), the [Carthaginians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthaginians "Carthaginians") expanded and established small settlements along the North African coast; by 600 BC, a [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenicia "Phoenicia") presence existed at [Tipasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tipasa "Tipasa"), east of [Cherchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherchell "Cherchell"), [Hippo Regius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippo_Regius "Hippo Regius") (modern [Annaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annaba "Annaba")) and [Rusicade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rusicade "Rusicade") (modern [Skikda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skikda "Skikda")). These settlements served as market towns as well as anchorages.

As Carthaginian power grew, its impact on the indigenous population increased dramatically. Berber civilisation was already at a stage in which agriculture, manufacturing, trade, and political organisation supported several states. Trade links between Carthage and the Berbers in the interior grew, but territorial expansion also resulted in the enslavement or military recruitment of some Berbers and in the extraction of tribute from others.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/GM_Massinissa.png/220px-GM_Massinissa.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:GM_Massinissa.png)

[Masinissa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masinissa "Masinissa") (c. 238–148 BC), first king of Numidia

By the early 4th century BC, Berbers formed the single largest element of the Carthaginian army. In the [Revolt of the Mercenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercenary_War "Mercenary War"), Berber soldiers rebelled from 241 to 238 BC after being unpaid following the defeat of Carthage in the [First Punic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Punic_War "First Punic War").[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fischer-Bovet2014-53) They succeeded in obtaining control of much of Carthage's North African territory, and they minted coins bearing the name Libyan, used in Greek to describe natives of North Africa. The Carthaginian state declined because of successive defeats by the Romans in the [Punic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punic_Wars "Punic Wars").[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Spielvogel2014-54)

In 146 BC the city of [Carthage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carthage "Carthage") was destroyed. As Carthaginian power waned, the influence of Berber leaders in the hinterland grew. By the 2nd century BC, several large but loosely administered Berber kingdoms had emerged. Two of them were established in [Numidia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numidia "Numidia"), behind the coastal areas controlled by Carthage. West of Numidia lay [Mauretania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauretania "Mauretania"), which extended across the [Moulouya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moulouya_River "Moulouya River") in modern-day [Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morocco "Morocco") to the Atlantic Ocean. The high point of Berber civilisation, unequalled until the coming of the [Almohads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohads "Almohads") and [Almoravids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravids "Almoravids") more than a millennium later, was reached during the reign of [Masinissa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masinissa "Masinissa") in the 2nd century BC.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Justinian555AD.png/220px-Justinian555AD.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Justinian555AD.png)

The lands which make up modern-day Algeria were part of [Byzantine North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_North_Africa "Byzantine North Africa") (The empire in 555 under [Justinian the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_the_Great "Justinian the Great"), at its greatest extent since the fall of the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire "Western Roman Empire") ([vassals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal_state "Vassal state") in pink))

After Masinissa's death in 148 BC, the Berber kingdoms were divided and reunited several times. Masinissa's line survived until 24 AD, when the remaining Berber territory was annexed to the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire "Roman Empire").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Side_face_%282%29.jpg/170px-Side_face_%282%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Side_face_\(2\).jpg)

Roman inscription from Agueneb in the province of [Laghouat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laghouat "Laghouat")

For several centuries Algeria was ruled by the Romans, who founded many colonies in the region. Algeria is home to the second-largest number of Roman sites and remains after Italy. Rome, after getting rid of its powerful rival Carthage in the year 146 BC, decided a century later to include Numidia to become the new master of North Africa. They built more than 500 cities.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55) Like the rest of North Africa, Algeria was one of the breadbaskets of the empire, exporting cereals and other agricultural products. [Saint Augustine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo "Augustine of Hippo") was the bishop of [Hippo Regius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippo_Regius "Hippo Regius") (modern-day Annaba, Algeria), located in the Roman province of [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa_\(Roman_province\) "Africa (Roman province)"). The Germanic [Vandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandals "Vandals") of [Geiseric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geiseric "Geiseric") moved into North Africa in 429, and by 435 controlled coastal Numidia.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vandaf-56) They did not make any significant settlement on the land, as they were harassed by local tribes.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*] In fact, by the time the Byzantines arrived [Leptis Magna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leptis_Magna "Leptis Magna") was abandoned and the Msellata region was occupied by the indigenous [Laguatan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazigh "Amazigh") who had been busy facilitating an [Amazigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amazigh "Amazigh") political, military and cultural revival.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-vandaf-56)[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-57) Furthermore, during the rule of the Romans, Byzantines, Vandals, Carthaginians, and Ottomans the Berber people were the only or one of the few in North Africa who remained independent.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58)[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Walmsley-1858-59)[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60)[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-61) The Berber people were so resistant that even during the Muslim conquest of North Africa they still had control and possession over their mountains.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-62)[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-63)

The collapse of the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire "Western Roman Empire") led to the establishment of a native Kingdom based in [Altava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altava "Altava") (modern-day Algeria) known as the [Mauro-Roman Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauro-Roman_Kingdom "Mauro-Roman Kingdom"). It was succeeded by another Kingdom based in Altava, the [Kingdom of Altava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Altava "Kingdom of Altava"). During the reign of [Kusaila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kusaila "Kusaila") its territory extended from the region of modern-day [Fez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fez,_Morocco "Fez, Morocco") in the west to the western [Aurès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aur%C3%A8s "Aurès") and later [Kairaouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kairouan "Kairouan") and the interior of Ifriqiya in the east.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64)[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-65)[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-66)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-67)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68)[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-69)

### Middle Ages

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Middle Ages")]

Main article: [Medieval Muslim Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Muslim_Algeria "Medieval Muslim Algeria")

After negligible resistance from the locals, [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslims "Muslims") [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs "Arabs") of the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate "Umayyad Caliphate") conquered Algeria in the early 8th century.

Large numbers of the indigenous Berber people converted to Islam. Christians, Berber and Latin speakers remained in the great majority in Tunisia until the end of the 9th century and Muslims only became a vast majority some time in the 10th.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jonathan-70) After the fall of the Umayyad Caliphate, numerous local dynasties emerged, including the [Rustamids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustamids "Rustamids"), [Aghlabids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aghlabids "Aghlabids"), [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimids "Fatimids"), [Zirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirids "Zirids"), [Hammadids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammadids "Hammadids"), [Almoravids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravids "Almoravids"), [Almohads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohads "Almohads") and the [Zayyanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdalwadid "Abdalwadid"). The Christians left in three waves: after the initial conquest, in the 10th century and the 11th. The last were evacuated to [Sicily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sicily "Sicily") by the [Normans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normans "Normans") and the few remaining died out in the 14th century.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-jonathan-70)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Fatimid.jpg/170px-Fatimid.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fatimid.jpg)

Fatimid Caliphate, a [Shia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Islam "Shia Islam") [Ismaili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma%27ilism "Isma'ilism") dynasty that ruled much of North Africa, c. 960–1100

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Statue_of_Dyhia_in_Khenchela_%28Algeria%29.jpg/170px-Statue_of_Dyhia_in_Khenchela_%28Algeria%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Statue_of_Dyhia_in_Khenchela_\(Algeria\).jpg)

[Dihya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dihya "Dihya") memorial in [Khenchela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khenchela "Khenchela"), Algeria

During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages "Middle Ages"), North Africa was home to many great scholars, saints and sovereigns including [Judah Ibn Quraysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judah_ibn_Kuraish "Judah ibn Kuraish"), the first grammarian to mention Semitic and Berber languages, the great [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism "Sufism") masters [Sidi Boumediene (Abu Madyan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Madyan "Abu Madyan") and [Sidi El Houari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidi_El_Houari "Sidi El Houari"), and the Emirs [Abd Al Mu'min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Mu%27min "Abd al-Mu'min") and [Yāghmūrasen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaghmurasen_Ibn_Zyan "Yaghmurasen Ibn Zyan"). It was during this time that the [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimids "Fatimids") or children of [Fatima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatima "Fatima"), daughter of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad "Muhammad"), came to the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb "Maghreb"). These "Fatimids" went on to found a long lasting dynasty stretching across the Maghreb, [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz "Hejaz") and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant "Levant"), boasting a secular inner government, as well as a powerful army and navy, made up primarily of [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs "Arabs") and [Levantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant "Levant") extending from Algeria to their capital state of [Cairo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo "Cairo"). The [Fatimid caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_caliphate "Fatimid caliphate") began to collapse when its governors the [Zirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirids "Zirids") seceded. To punish them the Fatimids sent the Arab [Banu Hilal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Hilal "Banu Hilal") and [Banu Sulaym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Sulaym "Banu Sulaym") against them. The resultant war is recounted in the epic [Tāghribāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taghribat_Bani_Hilal "Taghribat Bani Hilal"). In Al-Tāghrībāt the Amazigh Zirid Hero [Khālīfā Al-Zānatī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalifa_al-Zanati "Khalifa al-Zanati") asks daily, for duels, to defeat the Hilalan hero [Ābu Zayd al-Hilalī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Zayd_al-Hilali "Abu Zayd al-Hilali") and many other Arab knights in a string of victories. The [Zirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirid "Zirid"), however, were ultimately defeated ushering in an adoption of Arab customs and culture. The indigenous [Amazigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people "Berber people") tribes, however, remained largely independent, and depending on tribe, location and time controlled varying parts of the Maghreb, at times unifying it (as under the Fatimids). The Fatimid Islamic state, also known as [Fatimid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate "Fatimid Caliphate") made an Islamic empire that included North Africa, Sicily, [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\) "Palestine (region)"), [Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan "Jordan"), [Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanon "Lebanon"), [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria "Syria"), [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt "Egypt"), the [Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea "Red Sea") coast of Africa, Tihamah, [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz "Hejaz") and [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemen "Yemen").[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71)[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72)[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-73) Caliphates from Northern Africa traded with the other empires of their time, as well as forming part of a confederated support and trade network with other Islamic states during the Islamic Era.

The [Berber people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people "Berber people") historically consisted of several tribes. The two main branches were the Botr and Barnès tribes, who were divided into tribes, and again into sub-tribes. Each region of the Maghreb contained several tribes (for example, [Sanhadja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhadja "Sanhadja"), [Houara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houara "Houara"), [Zenata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenata "Zenata"), [Masmouda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masmuda "Masmuda"), [Kutama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutama "Kutama"), Awarba, and [Berghwata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berghwata "Berghwata")). All these tribes made independent territorial decisions.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-74)

Several [Amazigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_people "Berber people") dynasties emerged during the Middle Ages in the Maghreb and other nearby lands. [Ibn Khaldun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun "Ibn Khaldun") provides a table summarising the Amazigh dynasties of the Maghreb region, the [Zirid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirid_dynasty "Zirid dynasty"), [Ifranid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Tlemcen "Emirate of Tlemcen"), [Maghrawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrawid_Dynasty "Maghrawid Dynasty"), [Almoravid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravid "Almoravid"), [Hammadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammadid "Hammadid"), [Almohad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad "Almohad"), [Merinid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merinid "Merinid"), [Abdalwadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zayyanid_dynasty "Zayyanid dynasty"), [Wattasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wattasid "Wattasid"), [Meknassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meknassa "Meknassa") and [Hafsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafsid "Hafsid") dynasties.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75) Both of the [Hammadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammadid "Hammadid") and [Zirid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirid "Zirid") empires as well as the [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid "Fatimid") established their rule in all of the Maghreb countries. The [Zirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirid_dynasty "Zirid dynasty") ruled land in what is now Algeria, Tunisia, Morocco, Libya, Spain, Malta and Italy. The [Hammadids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammadid_dynasty "Hammadid dynasty") captured and held important regions such as Ouargla, Constantine, Sfax, Susa, Algiers, Tripoli and Fez establishing their rule in every country in the Maghreb region.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-76)[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77)[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78) The [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate "Fatimid Caliphate") which was created and established by the Kutama Berbers[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Nanjira-2010-79)[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Fage-1958-80) conquered all of North Africa as well as Sicily and parts of the Middle East.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Maghrawa_dynasty_-_dynastie_maghraoua.jpg/220px-Maghrawa_dynasty_-_dynastie_maghraoua.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Maghrawa_dynasty_-_dynastie_maghraoua.jpg)

Territories controlled by the [Maghrawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrawid_Dynasty "Maghrawid Dynasty")

Following the Berber revolt numerous independent states emerged across the Maghreb. In Algeria the [Rustamid Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rustamid_dynasty "Rustamid dynasty") was established. The Rustamid realm stretched from Tafilalt in Morocco to the Nafusa mountains in Libya including south, central and western Tunisia therefore including territory in all of the modern day Maghreb countries, in the south the Rustamid realm expanded to the modern borders of [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali "Mali") and included territory in [Mauritania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauritania "Mauritania").[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81)[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-82)[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-83)

Once extending their control over all of the Maghreb, part of Spain[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-84) and briefly over Sicily,[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85) originating from modern Algeria, the [Zirids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirids "Zirids") only controlled modern [Ifriqiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifriqiya "Ifriqiya") by the 11th century. The Zirids recognised nominal suzerainty of the Fatimid caliphs of Cairo. [El Mu'izz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mu%27izz_ibn_Badis "Al-Mu'izz ibn Badis") the Zirid ruler decided to end this recognition and declared his independence.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86)[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Meredith-2014-87) The Zirids also fought against other Zenata Kingdoms, for example the [Maghrawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrawa "Maghrawa"), a Berber dynasty originating from Algeria and which at one point was a dominant power in the Maghreb ruling over much of Morocco and western Algeria including Fez, [Sijilmasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sijilmasa "Sijilmasa"), [Aghmat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aghmat "Aghmat"), [Oujda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oujda "Oujda"), most of the Sous and Draa and reaching as far as M'sila and the Zab in Algeria.[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88)[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89)[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-90)[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91)

As the Fatimid state was at the time too weak to attempt a direct invasion, they found another means of revenge. Between the [Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile "Nile") and the [Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea "Red Sea") were living [Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouin "Bedouin") nomad tribes expelled from [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia "Arabia") for their disruption and turbulency. The [Banu Hilal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Hilal "Banu Hilal") and the [Banu Sulaym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Sulaym "Banu Sulaym") for example, who regularly disrupted farmers in the [Nile Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_Valley "Nile Valley") since the nomads would often loot their farms. The then [Fatimid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate "Fatimid Caliphate") vizier decided to destroy what he could not control, and broke a deal with the chiefs of these Bedouin tribes.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92) The Fatimids even gave them money to leave.

Whole tribes set off with women, children, elders, animals and camping equipment. Some stopped on the way, especially in [Cyrenaica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrenaica "Cyrenaica"), where they are still one of the essential elements of the settlement but most arrived in [Ifriqiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ifriqiya "Ifriqiya") by the [Gabes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gab%C3%A8s_Governorate "Gabès Governorate") region, arriving 1051.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93) The [Zirid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zirid "Zirid") ruler tried to stop this rising tide, but with each encounter, the last under the walls of [Kairouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kairouan "Kairouan"), his troops were defeated and the Arabs remained masters of the battlefield. The Arabs usually did not take control over the cities, instead looting them and destroying them.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Meredith-2014-87)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/Mansourah-1.jpg/220px-Mansourah-1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mansourah-1.jpg)

[Mansourah Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansourah_Mosque "Mansourah Mosque"), Tlemcen

The invasion kept going, and in 1057 the Arabs spread on the high plains of [Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine,_Algeria "Constantine, Algeria") where they encircled the [Qalaa of Banu Hammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beni_Hammad_Fort "Beni Hammad Fort") (capital of the [Hammadid Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammadid_dynasty "Hammadid dynasty")), as they had done in Kairouan a few decades ago. From there they gradually gained the upper [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers "Algiers") and [Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oran "Oran") plains. Some of these territories were forcibly taken back by the [Almohads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohads "Almohads") in the second half of the 12th century. The influx of [Bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedouin "Bedouin") tribes was a major factor in the linguistic, cultural [Arabisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabization "Arabization") of the Maghreb and in the spread of [nomadism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad "Nomad") in areas where agriculture had previously been dominant.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94) [Ibn Khaldun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun "Ibn Khaldun") noted that the lands ravaged by the [Banu Hilal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Hilal "Banu Hilal") tribes had become completely arid desert.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95)

The [Almohads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almohad_Caliphate "Almohad Caliphate") originating from modern day Morocco, although founded by a man originating from modern day Algeria[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) known as [Abd al-Mu'min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Mu%27min "Abd al-Mu'min") would soon take control over the Maghreb. During the time of the Almohad Dynasty [Abd al-Mu'min](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Mu%27min "Abd al-Mu'min")'s tribe, the Koumïa, were the main supporters of the throne and the most important body of the empire.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97) Defeating the weakening [Almoravid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almoravid_dynasty "Almoravid dynasty") and taking control over Morocco in 1147,[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-98) they pushed into Algeria in 1152, taking control over Tlemcen, Oran, and Algiers,[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Huebner-1996-99) wrestling control from the Hilian Arabs, and by the same year they defeated Hammadids who controlled Eastern Algeria.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Huebner-1996-99)

Following their decisive defeat in the [Battle of Las Navas de Tolosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Las_Navas_de_Tolosa "Battle of Las Navas de Tolosa") in 1212 the Almohads began collapsing, and in 1235 the governor of modern-day Western Algeria, [Yaghmurasen Ibn Zyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaghmurasen_Ibn_Zyan "Yaghmurasen Ibn Zyan") declared his independence and established the [Kingdom of Tlemcen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tlemcen "Kingdom of Tlemcen") and the [Zayyanid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zayyanid_dynasty "Zayyanid dynasty"). Warring with the Almohad forces attempting to restore control over Algeria for 13 years, they defeated the Almohads in 1248 after killing their Caliph in a successful ambush near Oujda.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Maghreb_15th_Century.svg/250px-Maghreb_15th_Century.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Maghreb_15th_Century.svg)

The [Zayyanid kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Tlemcen "Kingdom of Tlemcen") of Tlemcen in the fifteenth century and its neighbors

The Zayyanids retained their control over Algeria for 3 centuries. Much of the eastern territories of Algeria were under the authority of the [Hafsid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafsid_dynasty "Hafsid dynasty"),[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101) although the [Emirate of Bejaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafsids_of_Beja%C3%AFa "Hafsids of Bejaïa") encompassing the Algerian territories of the Hafsids would occasionally be independent from central Tunisian control. At their peak the Zayyanid kingdom included all of Morocco as its vassal to the west and in the east reached as far as [Tunis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunis "Tunis") which they captured during the reign of Abu Tashfin.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102)[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103)[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Constantine2-104)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Constantine-105)[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cour-106)[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Arnolet-107)

After several conflicts with local [Barbary pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbary_pirates "Barbary pirates") sponsored by the Zayyanid sultans,[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-108) Spain decided to invade Algeria and defeat the native Kingdom of Tlemcen. In 1505, they [invaded and captured Mers el Kébir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Mers-el-K%C3%A9bir_\(1505\) "Capture of Mers-el-Kébir (1505)"),[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-109) and in 1509 after a bloody siege, they [conquered Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_conquest_of_Oran_\(1509\) "Spanish conquest of Oran (1509)").[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-110) Following their decisive victories over the Algerians in the western-coastal areas of Algeria, the Spanish decided to get bolder, and invaded more Algerian cities. In 1510, they led a series of sieges and attacks, taking over Bejaia [in a large siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capture_of_Bejaia_\(1510\) "Capture of Bejaia (1510)"),[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-111) and leading a [semi-successful siege against Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Siege_of_Algiers_\(1510\)\&action=edit\&redlink=1 "Siege of Algiers (1510) (page does not exist)"). They also besieged Tlemcen. In 1511, they took control over [Cherchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherchell "Cherchell")[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112) and [Jijel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jijel "Jijel"), and attacked [Mostaganem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mostaganem "Mostaganem") where although they were not able to conquer the city, they were able to force a tribute on them.

### Early modern era

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Early modern era")]

Main article: [Regency of Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_of_Algiers "Regency of Algiers")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Barbarossa_Hayreddin_Pasha.jpg/170px-Barbarossa_Hayreddin_Pasha.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Barbarossa_Hayreddin_Pasha.jpg)

[Hayreddin Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayreddin_Barbarossa "Hayreddin Barbarossa")

In 1516, the Turkish privateer brothers [Aruj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aruj "Aruj") and [Hayreddin Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayreddin_Barbarossa "Hayreddin Barbarossa"), who operated successfully under the [Hafsids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafsids "Hafsids"), moved their base of operations to Algiers. They succeeded in conquering Jijel and Algiers from the [Spaniards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spain "Spain") with help from the locals who saw them as liberators from the Christians, but the brothers eventually assassinated the local noble Salim al-Tumi and took control over the city and the surrounding regions. Their state is known as the [Regency of Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regency_of_Algiers "Regency of Algiers"). When Aruj was killed in 1518 during his [invasion of Tlemcen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Tlemcen_\(1518\) "Fall of Tlemcen (1518)"), [Hayreddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayreddin_Barbarossa "Hayreddin Barbarossa") succeeded him as military commander of Algiers. The [Ottoman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire "Ottoman Empire") [sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_of_the_Ottoman_Empire "Sultan of the Ottoman Empire") gave him the title of [beylerbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beylerbey "Beylerbey") and a contingent of some 2,000 [janissaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janissaries "Janissaries"). With the aid of this force and native Algerians, Hayreddin conquered the whole area between Constantine and Oran (although the city of Oran remained in Spanish hands until 1792).[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-csa16-113)[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Mikaberidze-114)

The next beylerbey was Hayreddin's son [Hasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_Pasha_\(son_of_Barbarossa\) "Hasan Pasha (son of Barbarossa)"), who assumed the position in 1544. He was a [Kouloughli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kouloughlis "Kouloughlis") or of mixed origins, as his mother was an Algerian Mooresse.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115) Until 1587 Beylerbeylik of Algiers was governed by Beylerbeys who served terms with no fixed limits. Subsequently, with the institution of a regular administration, governors with the title of pasha ruled for three-year terms. The pasha was assisted by an autonomous janissary unit, known in Algeria as the [Ojaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odjak_of_Algiers "Odjak of Algiers") who were led by an [agha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agha_of_the_Janissaries "Agha of the Janissaries"). Discontent among the ojaq rose in the mid-1600s because they were not paid regularly, and they repeatedly revolted against the pasha. As a result, the agha charged the pasha with corruption and incompetence and seized power in 1659.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-csa16-113)

[Plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plague_\(disease\) "Plague (disease)") had repeatedly struck the cities of North Africa. Algiers lost between 30,000 and 50,000 inhabitants to the plague in 1620–21, and had high fatalities in 1654–57, 1665, 1691 and 1740–42.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Davis-116)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/North_Africa%2C_from_the_%22Physical_And_Political_Map_Of_Africa_..._1822%22.png/220px-North_Africa%2C_from_the_%22Physical_And_Political_Map_Of_Africa_..._1822%22.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:North_Africa,_from_the_%22Physical_And_Political_Map_Of_Africa_..._1822%22.png)

Map of the Regency of Algiers in early 19th century

The [Barbary pirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbary_pirates "Barbary pirates") preyed on Christian and other non-Islamic shipping in the western Mediterranean Sea.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Davis-116) The pirates often took the passengers and crew on the ships and sold them or used them as [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery "Slavery").[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Hannay_1911-117) They also did a brisk business in ransoming some of the captives. According to Robert Davis, from the 16th to 19th century, pirates captured 1 million to 1.25 million Europeans as slaves.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-barbary-118) They often made raids on European coastal towns to capture Christian slaves to sell at [slave markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_Ottoman_Empire "Slavery in the Ottoman Empire") in North Africa and other parts of the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire "Ottoman Empire").[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-119) In 1544, for example, [Hayreddin Barbarossa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayreddin_Barbarossa "Hayreddin Barbarossa") captured the island of [Ischia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ischia "Ischia"), taking 4,000 prisoners, and enslaved some 9,000 inhabitants of [Lipari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lipari "Lipari"), almost the entire population.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-120) In 1551, the Ottoman governor of Algiers, [Turgut Reis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turgut_Reis "Turgut Reis"), enslaved the entire population of the Maltese island of [Gozo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gozo "Gozo"). Barbary pirates often attacked the [Balearic Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balearic_Islands "Balearic Islands"). The threat was so severe that residents abandoned the island of [Formentera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formentera "Formentera").[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121) The introduction of broad-sail ships from the beginning of the 17th century allowed them to branch out into the Atlantic.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Auchterlonie2012-122)

In July 1627 two pirate ships from Algiers under the command of [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_people "Dutch people") pirate [Jan Janszoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Janszoon "Jan Janszoon") sailed as far as [Iceland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Iceland "History of Iceland"),[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-123) [raiding and capturing slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Abductions "Turkish Abductions").[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124)[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-125)[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-126) Two weeks earlier another pirate ship from [Salé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sale,_Morocco "Sale, Morocco") in [Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saadi_dynasty "Saadi dynasty") had also raided in Iceland. Some of the slaves brought to Algiers were later ransomed back to Iceland, but some chose to stay in Algeria. In 1629, pirate ships from Algeria raided the [Faroe Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faroe_Islands "Faroe Islands").[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-127)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Portrait_de_Baba_Ali%2C_Dey_d%27Alger.jpg/170px-Portrait_de_Baba_Ali%2C_Dey_d%27Alger.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Portrait_de_Baba_Ali,_Dey_d%27Alger.jpg)

[Baba Ali Chaouch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baba_Ali_Chaouch "Baba Ali Chaouch"), Dey of Algiers

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Niels_simonsen_Alger_2.jpg/220px-Niels_simonsen_Alger_2.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Niels_simonsen_Alger_2.jpg)

Algiers, by [Niels Simonsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niels_Simonsen "Niels Simonsen")

In 1659, the Janissaries stationed in Algiers, also known commonly as the [Odjak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odjak_of_Algiers "Odjak of Algiers") of Algiers; and the Reis or the company of corsair captains rebelled, they removed the Ottoman [viceroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viceroy "Viceroy") from power, and placed one of its own in power. The new leader received the title of "Agha" then "[Dey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dey "Dey")" in 1671, and the right to select passed to the [divan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divan "Divan"), a council of some sixty military senior officers. Thus Algiers became a sovereign military republic. It was at first dominated by the odjak; but by the 18th century, it had become the dey's instrument. Although Algiers remained nominally part of the Ottoman Empire,[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-csa16-113) in reality they acted independently from the rest of the Empire,[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-128)[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-129) and often had wars with other Ottoman subjects and territories such as the [Beylik of Tunis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beylik_of_Tunis "Beylik of Tunis").[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-130)

The [dey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dey "Dey") was in effect a constitutional autocrat. The dey was elected for a life term, but in the 159 years (1671–1830) that the system was in place, fourteen of the twenty-nine deys were assassinated. Despite usurpation, military coups and occasional mob rule, the day-to-day operation of the Deylikal government was remarkably orderly. Although the regency patronised the tribal chieftains, it never had the unanimous allegiance of the countryside, where heavy taxation frequently provoked unrest. Autonomous tribal states were tolerated, and the regency's authority was seldom applied in the [Kabylia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabylie "Kabylie"),[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-csa16-113) although in 1730 the Regency was able to take control over the [Kingdom of Kuku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kuku "Kingdom of Kuku") in western Kabylia.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-131) Many cities in the northern parts of the Algerian desert paid taxes to Algiers or one of its Beys.[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-132)

Barbary raids in the Mediterranean continued to attack Spanish merchant shipping, and as a result, the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire "Spanish Empire") launched an [invasion in 1775](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Algiers_\(1775\) "Invasion of Algiers (1775)"), then the [Spanish Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Navy "Spanish Navy") bombarded Algiers in [1783](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardment_of_Algiers_\(1783\) "Bombardment of Algiers (1783)") and [1784](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardment_of_Algiers_\(1784\) "Bombardment of Algiers (1784)").[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Mikaberidze-114) For the attack in 1784, the Spanish fleet was to be joined by ships from such traditional enemies of Algiers as [Naples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Naples "Kingdom of Naples"), [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal "Kingdom of Portugal") and the [Knights of Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Hospitaller "Knights Hospitaller"). Over 20,000 cannonballs were fired, but all these military campaigns were doomed and Spain had to ask for peace in 1786 and paid 1 million pesos to the Dey.

In 1792, Algiers took back Oran and Mers el Kébir, the two last Spanish strongholds in Algeria.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-133) In the same year, they conquered the Moroccan [Rif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rif "Rif") and [Oujda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oujda "Oujda"), which they then abandoned in 1795.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/De_Engels-Nederlandse_vloot_in_de_Baai_van_Algiers_ter_ondersteuning_van_het_ultimatum_tot_vrijlating_van_blanke_slaven%2C_26_augustus_1816._Rijksmuseum_SK-A-1377.jpeg/220px-thumbnail.jpeg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:De_Engels-Nederlandse_vloot_in_de_Baai_van_Algiers_ter_ondersteuning_van_het_ultimatum_tot_vrijlating_van_blanke_slaven,_26_augustus_1816._Rijksmuseum_SK-A-1377.jpeg)

[Bombardment of Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardment_of_Algiers_\(1816\) "Bombardment of Algiers (1816)") by the Anglo-Dutch fleet, to support the ultimatum to release European slaves, August 1816

In the 19th century, Algerian pirates forged affiliations with Caribbean powers, paying a "license tax" in exchange for safe harbor of their vessels.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135)

Attacks by Algerian pirates on American merchantmen resulted in the [First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Barbary_War "First Barbary War") and [Second Barbary Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Barbary_War "Second Barbary War"), which ended the attacks on U.S. ships in 1815. A year later, a combined [Anglo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland "United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland")-[Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_the_Netherlands "United Kingdom of the Netherlands") fleet, under the command of [Lord Exmouth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Pellew,_1st_Viscount_Exmouth "Edward Pellew, 1st Viscount Exmouth") [bombarded Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombardment_of_Algiers_\(1816\) "Bombardment of Algiers (1816)") to stop similar attacks on European fishermen. These efforts proved successful, although Algerian piracy would continue until the [French conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_conquest_of_Algeria "French conquest of Algeria") in 1830.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136)

### French colonisation (1830–1962)

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: French colonisation (1830–1962)")]

Main articles: [French Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Algeria "French Algeria"), [Pacification of Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacification_of_Algeria "Pacification of Algeria"), and [Algerian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War "Algerian War")

See also: [French North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_North_Africa "French North Africa")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Vernet-Combat_de_Somah.jpg/220px-Vernet-Combat_de_Somah.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vernet-Combat_de_Somah.jpg)

Battle of Somah in 1836

Under the pretext of a slight to their consul, the [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Monarchy "July Monarchy") invaded and [captured Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Algiers_in_1830 "Invasion of Algiers in 1830") in 1830.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-137)[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-138) According to several historians, the methods used by the French to establish control over Algeria reached [genocidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_genocide "Algerian genocide") proportions.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139)[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-140)[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-141) Historian [Ben Kiernan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Kiernan "Ben Kiernan") wrote on the French conquest of Algeria: "By 1875, the French conquest was complete. The war had killed approximately 825,000 indigenous Algerians since 1830".[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-142) French losses from 1831 to 1851 were 92,329 dead in the hospital and only 3,336 killed in action.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-143)[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-144) In 1872, The Algerian population stood at about 2.9 million.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-145)\[*[unreliable source?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources "Wikipedia:Reliable sources")*] French policy was predicated on "civilising" the country.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-146) The slave trade and piracy in Algeria ceased following the French conquest.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Hannay_1911-117) The [conquest of Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_conquest_of_Algeria "French conquest of Algeria") by the French took some time and resulted in considerable bloodshed. A combination of violence and disease epidemics caused the [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples "Indigenous peoples") Algerian population to decline by nearly one-third from 1830 to 1872.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-147)[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Ricoux1880-148)\[*[unreliable source?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources "Wikipedia:Reliable sources")*] On 17 September 1860, [Napoleon III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon_III "Napoleon III") declared "Our first duty is to take care of the happiness of the three million Arabs, whom the fate of arms has brought under our domination."[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-149) During this time, only Kabylia resisted, the Kabylians were not colonised until after the [Mokrani Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mokrani_Revolt "Mokrani Revolt") in 1871.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

[Alexis de Tocqueville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_de_Tocqueville "Alexis de Tocqueville") wrote and never completed an unpublished essay outlining his ideas for how to transform Algeria from an occupied tributary state to a colonial regime, wherein he advocated for a mixed system of "total domination and total colonisation" whereby French military would wage total war against civilian populations while a colonial administration would provide rule of law and property rights to settlers within French occupied cities.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Tocqueville_2023,_pp.47-150)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Abd_al-Qadir.jpg/170px-Abd_al-Qadir.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Abd_al-Qadir.jpg)

[Emir Abdelkader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdelkader_El_Djezairi "Abdelkader El Djezairi"), Algerian leader insurgent against French colonial rule, 1865

From 1848 until independence, France administered the whole Mediterranean region of Algeria as an integral part and *département* of the nation. One of France's longest-held overseas territories, Algeria became a destination for hundreds of thousands of [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_ethnic_groups "European ethnic groups") immigrants, who became known as *colons* and later, as *[Pied-Noirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied-Noir "Pied-Noir").* Between 1825 and 1847, 50,000 French people emigrated to Algeria.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-151)[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-152) These settlers benefited from the French government's confiscation of communal land from tribal peoples, and the application of modern agricultural techniques that increased the amount of arable land.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-153) Many Europeans settled in [Oran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oran "Oran") and [Algiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers "Algiers"), and by the early 20th century they formed a majority of the population in both cities.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Six_chefs_FLN_-_1954.jpg/220px-Six_chefs_FLN_-_1954.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Six_chefs_FLN_-_1954.jpg)

The six historical Leaders of the FLN: [Rabah Bitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabah_Bitat "Rabah Bitat"), [Mostefa Ben Boulaïd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustapha_Benboula%C3%AFd "Mustapha Benboulaïd"), [Mourad Didouche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mourad_Didouche "Mourad Didouche"), [Mohammed Boudiaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Boudiaf "Mohammed Boudiaf"), [Krim Belkacem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krim_Belkacem "Krim Belkacem") and [Larbi Ben M'Hidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larbi_Ben_M%27Hidi "Larbi Ben M'Hidi").

During the late 19th and early 20th century, the [European share](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Africans_of_European_ancestry "White Africans of European ancestry") was almost a fifth of the population. The French government aimed at making Algeria an assimilated part of France, and this included substantial educational investments especially after 1900. The indigenous cultural and religious resistance heavily opposed this tendency, but in contrast to the other colonised countries' path in central Asia and [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus "Caucasus"), Algeria kept its individual skills and a relatively human-capital intensive agriculture.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-155)

During the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War"), Algeria came under [Vichy control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France "Vichy France") before being liberated by the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II "Allies of World War II") in [Operation Torch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Torch "Operation Torch"), which saw the first large-scale deployment of [American troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_United_States_during_World_War_II "Military history of the United States during World War II") in the [North African campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_African_campaign "North African campaign").[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-USMA-1945-156)

Gradually, dissatisfaction among the Muslim population, which lacked political and economic status under the colonial system, gave rise to demands for greater political autonomy and eventually independence from [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_the_French_Republic "Provisional Government of the French Republic"). In May 1945, the uprising against the occupying French forces was suppressed through what is now known as the [Sétif and Guelma massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9tif_and_Guelma_massacre "Sétif and Guelma massacre"). Tensions between the two population groups came to a head in 1954, when the first violent events of what was later called the [Algerian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_War "Algerian War") began after the publication of the [Declaration of 1 November 1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_1_November_1954 "Declaration of 1 November 1954"). Historians have estimated that between 30,000 and 150,000 [Harkis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harki "Harki") and their dependents were killed by the [National Liberation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Front_\(Algeria\) "National Liberation Front (Algeria)") (FLN) or by [lynch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching "Lynching") mobs in Algeria.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-157) The FLN used hit and run attacks in Algeria and France as part of its war, and the French conducted [severe reprisals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torture_during_the_Algerian_War_of_Independence "Torture during the Algerian War of Independence"). In addition, the French destroyed over 8,000 villages[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Kevin_Shillington-158) and relocated over 2 million Algerians to [concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment_camps_in_France#Algerian_War "Internment camps in France").[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159)

The war led to the death of hundreds of thousands of Algerians and hundreds of thousands of injuries. Historians, like [Alistair Horne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alistair_Horne "Alistair Horne") and [Raymond Aron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Aron "Raymond Aron"), state that the actual number of Algerian Muslim war dead was far greater than the original FLN and official French estimates but was less than the 1 million deaths claimed by the Algerian government after independence. Horne estimated Algerian casualties during the span of eight years to be around 700,000.[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-160) The war uprooted more than 2 million Algerians.[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-161)

The war against French rule concluded in 1962, when Algeria gained complete independence following the March 1962 [Evian agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evian_agreements "Evian agreements") and the July 1962 [self-determination referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_self-determination_referendum,_1962 "Algerian self-determination referendum, 1962").

### The first three decades of independence (1962–1991)

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: The first three decades of independence (1962–1991)")]

Main article: [History of Algeria (1962–1999)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Algeria_\(1962%E2%80%931999\) "History of Algeria (1962–1999)")

The number of European *[Pied-Noirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pied-Noir "Pied-Noir")* who fled Algeria totaled more than 900,000 between 1962 and 1964.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-162) The exodus to mainland France accelerated after the [Oran massacre of 1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oran_massacre_of_1962 "Oran massacre of 1962"), in which hundreds of militants entered European sections of the city and began attacking civilians.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Houari_Boumediene%27s_Portrait.jpg/140px-Houari_Boumediene%27s_Portrait.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Houari_Boumediene%27s_Portrait.jpg)

[Houari Boumediene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houari_Boumediene "Houari Boumediene")

Algeria's first president was the Front de Libération Nationale ([FLN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FLN_\(Algeria\) "FLN (Algeria)")) leader [Ahmed Ben Bella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Ben_Bella "Ahmed Ben Bella"). Morocco's claim to [portions of western Algeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Morocco "Greater Morocco") led to the [Sand War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_War "Sand War") in 1963. Ben Bella was [overthrown in 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_Algerian_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat "1965 Algerian coup d'état") by [Houari Boumédiène](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houari_Boum%C3%A9di%C3%A8ne "Houari Boumédiène"), his former ally and defence minister. Under Ben Bella, the government had become increasingly [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism "Socialism") and authoritarian; Boumédienne continued this trend. However, he relied much more on the army for his support, and reduced the sole legal party to a symbolic role. He [collectivised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_farming "Collective farming") agriculture and launched a massive industrialisation drive. [Oil extraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraction_of_petroleum "Extraction of petroleum") facilities were nationalised. This was especially beneficial to the leadership after the international [1973 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis "1973 oil crisis").

Boumédienne's successor, [Chadli Bendjedid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chadli_Bendjedid "Chadli Bendjedid"), introduced some liberal economic reforms. He promoted a policy of [Arabisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabisation "Arabisation") in Algerian society and public life. Teachers of Arabic, brought in from other Muslim countries, spread conventional Islamic thought in schools and sowed the seeds of a return to Orthodox Islam.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-faco-163)

The Algerian economy became increasingly dependent on oil, leading to hardship when the price collapsed during the [1980s oil glut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980s_oil_glut "1980s oil glut").[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Prochaska-2013-164) Economic recession caused by the crash in world oil prices resulted in Algerian social unrest during the 1980s; by the end of the decade, Bendjedid introduced a multi-party system. Political parties developed, such as the [Islamic Salvation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Salvation_Front "Islamic Salvation Front") (FIS), a broad coalition of Muslim groups.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-faco-163)

### Civil War (1991–2002) and aftermath

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Algeria\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Civil War (1991–2002) and aftermath")]

Main article: [Algerian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_Civil_War "Algerian Civil War")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Algerian_massacres_1997-1998.png/220px-Algerian_massacres_1997-1998.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Algerian_massacres_1997-1998.png)

[Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_during_the_Algerian_Civil_War "List of massacres during the Algerian Civil War") of over 50 people in 1997–1998. The [Armed Islamic Group (GIA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Islamic_Group "Armed Islamic Group") claimed responsibility for many of them.

In December 1991 the [Islamic Salvation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Salvation_Front "Islamic Salvation Front") dominated the first of two rounds of [legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algerian_legislative_election,_1991 "Algerian legislative election, 1991"). Fearing the election of an Islamist government, the authorities intervened on 11 January 1992, cancelling the elections. Bendjedid resigned and a [High Council of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Council_of_State_\(Algeria\) "High Council of State (Algeria)") was installed to act as the Presidency. It banned the FIS, triggering a civil [insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurgency "Insurgency") between the Front's armed wing, the [Armed Islamic Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Islamic_Group_of_Algeria "Armed Islamic Group of Algeria"), and the national armed forces, in which more than 100,000 people are thought to have died.
